\tau\in(0,1)
,
(\beta,\gamma,\kappa,\upsilon,\lambda,C_{\mathrm{App}})\in(0,1]\times(0,\infty)^{2}\times(0,1)\times[1,\infty)^{2}
. Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{H\ddot{o}l}}(\beta,\lambda)\cap\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{App}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{hpr}},\kappa,\gamma,\tau,C_{\mathrm{App}})
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
with regression function
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,1]
and with a Lebesgue absolutely continuous marginal
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with continuous density
f_{\mu}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\upsilon}(\mu)
, that
\mu\bigl(\eta^{-1}((\tau,1])\bigr)>0
and that
\eta^{-1}(\{\tau\})\neq\emptyset
. Then
\beta\gamma(\kappa-1)\leq d\kappa
.

Note that since
\mu\bigl(\eta^{-1}((\tau,1])\bigr)>0
and
\eta
is continuous, we must have
M_{\tau}>0
. Take
\Delta\in\bigl(0,\{M_{\tau}/(2C_{\mathrm{App}})\}^{1/\gamma}\bigr)
, and write
\omega:=\omega_{\mu,d}
for the lower density of
\mu
. Since
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{App}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{hpr}},\kappa,\gamma,\tau,C_{\mathrm{App}})
, we may take
A_{\Delta}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{hpr}}\cap\mathrm{Pow}\bigl(\mathcal{X}_{\Delta^{\gamma/\kappa}}({\omega})\cap\mathcal{X}_{\tau+\Delta}(\eta)\bigr)
with
\mu(A_{\Delta})\geq M_{\tau}-2C_{\mathrm{App}}\cdot\Delta^{\gamma}>0
. Now
A_{\Delta}
is a non-empty, compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\eta^{-1}(\{\tau\})
is a non-empty closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so we may choose
x_{0}\in\eta^{-1}(\{\tau\})
and
z_{0}\in A_{\Delta}
such that

\displaystyle\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|_{\infty}=\inf_{x\in\eta^{-1}(\{\tau\})}\inf_{z\in A_{\Delta}}\|x-z\|_{\infty}.

Let
A_{\Delta}^{\sharp}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x-z\|_{\infty}\leq\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|_{\infty}\text{ for some }z\in A_{\Delta}\}
, and note that
A_{\Delta}^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{hpr}}\cap\mathrm{Pow}\bigl(\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta)\bigr)
, so
\mu(A_{\Delta}^{\sharp})\leq M_{\tau}
and
\mu(A_{\Delta}^{\sharp}\setminus A_{\Delta})\leq 2C_{\mathrm{App}}\cdot\Delta^{\gamma}
. In addition, since
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{H\ddot{o}l}}(\beta,\lambda)
, we have
\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|_{\infty}\geq(\Delta/\lambda)^{1/\beta}=:3r_{\Delta}
. Hence, if we take

w_{0}:=z_{0}+\biggl(1+\frac{\upsilon}{2}\biggr)\cdot r_{\Delta}\cdot\frac{x_{0}-z_{0}}{\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|_{\infty}},

we have
\bar{B}_{r_{\Delta}}(w_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta)\cap(A_{\Delta}^{\sharp}\setminus A_{\Delta})
and
z_{0}\in B_{(1+\upsilon)\cdot r_{\Delta}}(w_{0})
. Thus, as
f_{\mu}(z_{0})\geq 2^{-d}\cdot\omega(z_{0})\geq 2^{-d}\cdot\Delta^{\gamma/\kappa}
and
w_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\upsilon}(\mu)
and
r_{\Delta}\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle 2C_{\mathrm{App}}\cdot\Delta^{\gamma}\geq\mu(A_{\Delta}^{\sharp}\setminus A_{\Delta})\geq\mu\bigl(\bar{B}_{r_{\Delta}}(w_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\upsilon\cdot r_{\Delta}^{d}\cdot\sup_{x^{\prime}\in B_{(1+\upsilon)r_{\Delta}}(w_{0})}f_{\mu}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\upsilon\cdot r_{\Delta}^{d}\cdot f_{\mu}(z_{0})\geq\frac{\upsilon}{6^{d}}\cdot\biggl(\frac{\Delta}{\lambda}\biggr)^{d/\beta}\cdot\Delta^{\gamma/\kappa}.

Letting
\Delta\searrow 0
we deduce that
\beta\gamma(\kappa-1)\leq d\kappa
.
∎

S8
Auxiliary results

S8.1
Disintegration and measure-theoretic preliminaries

Suppose we have a pair of measurable spaces
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{Y})
along with a probability distribution
P
on the product space
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{Y})
. Let
\mu
denote the marginal distribution of
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
. We say that
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
is a
disintegration
of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
if

1.

P_{x}
is a probability measure on
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{Y})
, for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
;

2.

x\mapsto P_{x}(B)
is a
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-measurable function, for every
B\in\mathcal{G}_{Y}
;

3.

P(A\times B)=\int_{A}P_{x}(B)\,d\mu(x)
for all
A\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
and
B\in\mathcal{G}_{Y}
.

We will make use of the following existence result: recall that a topological space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
is said to be
Polish
if there exists a metric
d_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
that induces the topology
\mathcal{T}_{X}
and for which
(\mathcal{X},d_{X})
is a complete, separable metric space.

Lemma S34
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{Y})
are Polish spaces with their corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebras. Let
P
be a probability distribution on
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{Y})
, with
\mu
denoting the marginal distribution of
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
. Then there exists a disintegration
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
with the property that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP(x,y)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y)\biggr)\,d\mu(x),

(S39)

for every
P
-integrable function
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the disintegration
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
is unique in the sense that if there exists another disintegration
(\tilde{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
, then
\tilde{P}_{x}=P_{x}
for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

This follows by combining Theorems 10.2.1 and 10.2.2 of
Dudley 2018
.
∎

A disintegration has the following useful interpretation. Suppose we have a pair of random variables
(X,Y)
taking values in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
with joint distribution
P
on
\mathcal{G}_{X}\times\mathcal{G}_{Y}
, where
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{G}_{Y})
are Polish spaces with their corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebras. Let
\mu
be the marginal on
\mathcal{X}
and
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
be a disintegration of
P
into conditional distributions. Then for all
P
-integrable functions
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(g(X,Y)\mid X=x\big)=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y),

(S40)

for
\mu
almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Indeed, by Lemma
S34
we see that
x\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y)
is a
\mu
-integrable function, and hence
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-measurable. Moreover, given any
A\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y)\biggr)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y)\biggr)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot g(x,y)\,dP(x,y)=\int_{A\times\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP(x,y),

where the second equality follows from (
S39
) with
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot g(x,y)
in place of
g(x,y)
.

Recall that for Borel subsets
B_{0}
,
B_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write
B_{0}\subseteq B_{1}
if
\mu(B_{0}\setminus B_{1})=0
and
B_{0}\not\subseteq B_{1}
if
\mu(B_{0}\setminus B_{1})>0
.

Lemma S35
.

Suppose that
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,1]
are regression functions for a Borel probability distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
with marginal probability distribution
\mu
. Then,
\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\eta_{0}(x)\neq\eta_{1}(x)\})=0
. Hence, given
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{0})
if and only if
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{1})
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, let
B_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\eta_{0}(x)\geq\eta_{1}(x)+\epsilon\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\epsilon\cdot\mu(B_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{\epsilon}}\{\eta_{0}(x)-\eta_{1}(x)\}\,d\mu=\int_{B_{\epsilon}\times[0,1]}y\,dP(x,y)-\int_{B_{\epsilon}\times[0,1]}y\,dP(x,y)=0,

so
\mu(B_{\epsilon})=0
. By taking a countable union we see that
\mu\bigl(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\eta_{0}(x)>\eta_{1}(x)\}\bigr)=0
, so by symmetry we have
\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\eta_{0}(x)\neq\eta_{1}(x)\})=0
. Thus, given
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
\mu(A\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{0}))=\mu(A\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{1}))
, so
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{0})
if and only if
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\tau}(\eta_{1})
.
∎

S8.2
Concentration results

We will require the following classic result that gives a uniform concentration inequality over classes of finite Vapnik–Chervonenkis dimension; we state it for distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for simplicity.

Lemma S36
(Vapnik–Chervonenkis concentration).

Let
\mu
be a probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mu
, with corresponding empirical distribution
\hat{\mu}_{n}
. There exists a universal constant
C_{\mathrm{VC}}>0
such that for any collection of sets
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1\leq\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{VC}}(\mathcal{S})<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl(\sup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\big|\hat{\mu}_{n}(S)-\mu(S)\big|\biggr)\leq C_{\mathrm{VC}}~\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{VC}}(\mathcal{S})}{n}}.

Moreover, for all
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\big|\hat{\mu}_{n}(S)-\mu(S)\big|>C_{\mathrm{VC}}~\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{VC}}(\mathcal{S})}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2n}}\biggr)\leq\delta.

For the expectation bound, see
Vershynin 2018
. The high-probability bound follows by McDiarmid’s inequality
(
Vershynin 2018
, Theorem 2.9.1)
.
∎The following lemma is used in the proof of Lemma
S38
.

Lemma S37
(
Garivier and Cappé 2011
).

Let
(Z_{j})_{j\in[m]}
be independent random variables taking values in
[0,1]
with
\max_{j\in[m]}\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}]\leq t
for some
t\in(0,1)
. Writing
\bar{Z}:=m^{-1}\sum_{j\in[m]}Z_{j}
, we have for
\kappa\in(t,1)
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{Z}\geq\kappa)\leq e^{-m\cdot\mathrm{kl}(\kappa,t)}.

By Jensen’s inequality, for
\theta>0
and
j\in[m]
,

\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta\cdot Z_{j}})\leq 1-\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})+e^{\theta}\cdot\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\leq 1+t(e^{\theta}-1).

Hence, by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{Z}\geq\kappa)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-m\theta\kappa}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta\cdot Z_{j}})\leq\bigl[e^{-\theta\kappa}\bigl\{1+t(e^{\theta}-1)\bigr\}\bigr]^{m}.

The lemma follows on taking
\theta=\log\bigl(\frac{\kappa(1-{t})}{{t}(1-\kappa)}\bigr)>0
.
∎

Lemma S38
.

Let
(Z_{j})_{j\in[m]}
be independent random variables taking values in
[0,1]
with
\max_{j\in[m]}\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\leq t
for some
t\in(0,1)
. Let
\bar{Z}:=m^{-1}\sum_{j\in[m]}Z_{j}
. Then for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bar{Z}\geq t+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{2m}}\biggr)\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl\{\mathrm{kl}(\bar{Z},t)\geq\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{m}\biggr\}\bigcap\{\bar{Z}>t\}\biggr)\leq\alpha.

The first inequality follows from the fact that

2(\bar{Z}-t)^{2}=2\mathrm{TV}^{2}\bigl(\mathrm{Bern}(\bar{Z}),\mathrm{Bern}(t)\bigr)\leq\mathrm{kl}(\bar{Z},t),

by Pinsker’s inequality. To prove the second inequality we begin by noting that
w\mapsto\mathrm{kl}(w,t)
is continuous and strictly increasing on the interval
[t,1]
, and consider two cases. If
\alpha\in\bigl(0,e^{-m\cdot\mathrm{kl}(1,t)}\bigr)
and
\bar{Z}>t
, then

\mathrm{kl}(\bar{Z},t)\leq\mathrm{kl}(1,t)<\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{m}.

On the other hand, if
\alpha\in\bigl[e^{-m\cdot\mathrm{kl}(1,t)},1\bigr)
, then by the intermediate value theorem we can find
\kappa_{\alpha}\in[t,1]
such that
\mathrm{kl}(\kappa_{\alpha},t)=m^{-1}\cdot\log(1/\alpha)
. Then by Lemma
S37
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl\{\mathrm{kl}(\bar{Z},t)\geq\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{m}\biggr\}\bigcap\{\bar{Z}>t\}\biggr)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bar{Z}\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\bigr)\leq e^{-m\cdot\mathrm{kl}(\kappa_{\alpha},t)}=\alpha,

as required.
∎In addition we shall make use of the following Chernoff bounds.

Lemma S39
(Multiplicative Chernoff — Theorem 2.3(b,c) of
McDiarmid 1998
).

Let
(Z_{j})_{j\in[m]}
be a sequence of independent random variables taking values in
[0,1]
. Then given any
\theta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}Z_{j}\leq(1-\theta)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\biggl(-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\biggr)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}Z_{j}\geq(1+\theta)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\biggl(-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2(1+\theta/3)}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{j})\biggr).

Lemma S40
(Multiplicative matrix Chernoff – Theorem 1.1 of
Tropp 2012
).

Let
(\mathbf{Z}_{j})_{j\in[m]}
be independent, non-negative definite
q\times q
matrices with
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{Z}_{j})\leq a_{\max}
almost surely, for every
j\in[m]
. Then, writing
a_{\min}:=m^{-1}\cdot\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\sum_{j\in[m]}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\bigr)
, we have for every
\theta\in[0,1]
that

\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\lambda_{\min}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\biggr)\leq m(1-\theta)a_{\min}\biggr\}\leq q\cdot\biggl(\frac{e^{-\theta}}{(1-\theta)^{1-\theta}}\biggr)^{ma_{\min}/a_{\max}}\leq q\cdot e^{-\frac{\theta^{2}ma_{\min}}{2a_{\max}}}.

S8.3
Useful lemmas for the lower bounds

We shall make use of the following result from
Brown and Low 1996
.

Lemma S41
(Brown–Low constrained risk inequality).

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
be probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
such that
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
, and assume that

\displaystyle I:=\chi^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{2},\mathbb{Q}_{1})+1<\infty.

Let
\epsilon\in\bigl(0,I^{-1/2}\bigr)
and let
Z:\Omega\rightarrow\{1,2\}
be a
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variable with
\mathbb{Q}_{1}(Z=2)\leq\epsilon^{2}
. Then
\mathbb{Q}_{2}(Z=1)\geq\bigl(1-\epsilon\sqrt{I}\bigr)^{2}
.

The following version of Fano’s lemma is a minor variant of
Gerchinovitz, Ménard and
Stoltz 2020
.

Lemma S42
(Fano’s lemma for
\chi^{2}
divergences).

Let
\mathbb{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{P}_{M},\mathbb{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Q}_{M}
denote probability measures on

(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
, and let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{M}\in\mathcal{A}
. Write
\bar{p}:=M^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{j}(A_{j})
and
\bar{q}:=M^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{Q}_{j}(A_{j})
. If
\bar{q}\in(0,1)
, then

\bar{p}\leq\bar{q}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{j},\mathbb{Q}_{j})\cdot\bar{q}(1-\bar{q})}.

In particular, if
M\geq 2
and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{M}
form a partition of

\Omega
, then

\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{j}(A_{j})\leq\frac{1}{M}+\sqrt{\inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{j},\mathbb{Q})\cdot\frac{1}{M}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{M}\biggr)},

where
\mathcal{Q}
denotes the set of all probability distributions on
\Omega
.

By the joint convexity of
\chi^{2}
divergence, together with the data processing inequality
(e.g.
Gerchinovitz, Ménard and
Stoltz 2020
, Lemma 2.1)
, we have

\frac{(\bar{p}-\bar{q})^{2}}{\bar{q}(1-\bar{q})}=\chi^{2}\bigl(\mathrm{Bern}(\bar{p}),\mathrm{Bern}(\bar{q})\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\chi^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{j}(A_{j}),\mathbb{Q}_{j}(A_{j})\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{j},\mathbb{Q}_{j}).

The first result follows on rearranging this inequality, and the second follows by taking
\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\cdots=\mathbb{Q}_{M}=\mathbb{Q}
and then taking an infimum over
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}
.
∎
